      "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nEC-HTE requires $n_{\\text{expert}} \\times P(Z_{\\text{minority}}) / K \\geq 25$ per subgroup for stable hierarchical Bayesian estimation.\nFor $K{=}2$, EC-HTE remains effective even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:classimbalance}); for $K{=}4$, $P(Z{=}1) \\geq 0.3$ is needed to approach breakeven with global correction at $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}250$.\nEven above this threshold, stratification incurs a variance cost: at $K{=}4$, extreme heterogeneity, and $n_{\\text{expert}} \\leq 100$, CausalForest RMSE reaches 3.5$\\times$ that of the naive estimator.\nThe method also requires moderate baseline classifier accuracy per subgroup; when any subgroup's FNR or FPR approaches 0.5, the confusion matrix row approaches a uniform distribution and inversion amplifies noise (Appendix~\\ref{app:semireal}), as confirmed independently in the hate speech case study (Appendix~\\ref{app:hatespeech}).\nThese failure modes interact: high per-subgroup error-rate variance combined with low minority prevalence $P(Z{=}1)$ jointly reduces effective per-subgroup expert labels for the positive class, compounding both sources of estimation error.\nAdditionally, our main semi-synthetic experiments use constant treatment effects across subgroups; a supplementary heterogeneous-$\\tau$ DGP (Appendix~\\ref{app:multilm_opensource}) shows that EC-HTE achieves targeted bias and RMSE improvement in subgroups with large $\\Delta\\tau$, though aggregate RMSE remains comparable to global correction.\nAt $K{=}8$ with $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}250$ (${\\approx}31$ labels/subgroup), EC-HTE maintains nominal coverage (95.6\\%) but RMSE exceeds global correction, establishing $K{=}8$ as a scalability boundary under fixed budget (Appendix~\\ref{app:k8_subgroups}).\nThe hierarchical prior implicitly performs soft merging---subgroups with insufficient data are shrunk toward the global estimate---but explicit hierarchical subgroup merging or adaptive budget allocation may extend the applicability frontier beyond $K{=}8$; we leave these extensions to future work.\n\nAssumption~(A1) (conditional independence of misclassification and potential outcomes) is not empirically testable.\nWhen LLM accuracy correlates with outcome magnitude---e.g., extreme sentiment is both easier to classify and associated with larger treatment effects---Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity} acquires an $O(\\gamma)$ perturbation (Section~\\ref{sec:theory}).\nSensitivity simulation (Appendix~\\ref{app:a1_sensitivity}) shows that EC-HTE maintains near-nominal coverage ($\\geq$93.5\\%) under outcome-dependent misclassification up to $\\gamma = 0.2$, with bias increasing only 13.7\\%; developing a formal closed-form bound for the $O(\\gamma)$ perturbation remains a natural extension~\\cite{lash2009applying,dorn2022sharp}.\nTreatment-dependent misclassification ($P(\\hat{Z} \\mid Z, T) \\neq P(\\hat{Z} \\mid Z)$), where LLM accuracy varies with treatment status, violates (A1) analogously; the $O(\\gamma)$ perturbation structure applies symmetrically, and sensitivity analysis for this case is left to future work.\n\nOracle experiments confirm CausalForest plug-in SE is well-calibrated (94--96\\% coverage with true $Z$ labels); EC-HTE plug-in SE achieves 93.8\\% aggregate coverage under standard hyperparameters ($n_{\\text{estimators}}{=}200$), with residual under-coverage concentrated in high-error subgroups due to asymmetric $M^{-1}$ SE propagation.\nA full bootstrap procedure ($B{=}100$; re-estimate CM and re-fit per replicate) recovers $\\geq$95\\% coverage across all expert budgets tested ($n_{\\text{expert}} \\in \\{25, 50, 75, 100, 200\\}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_se}); even plug-in SE achieves $\\geq$91\\% at $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}25$.\nFor the DIM estimator, plug-in SE achieves $\\geq$95\\% coverage across all budgets.\n\nThe diagnostic (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}) achieves AUC $= 0.86$ under synthetic symmetric errors but degrades to $0.63$ on real LLM confusion matrices (pooled across 16 model--dataset combinations; Appendix~\\ref{app:screening}).\nFor $K{=}2$, the diagnostic anti-predicts because $|\\delta_0|{=}|\\delta_1|$ forces score differentiation onto $1/\\pi_s$ alone, which inversely correlates with actual harm; for binary $Z$, the asymmetric $B_s$-based formulation (Remark~2) is algebraically equivalent and offers no improvement.\nPerformance is strongest for $K{\\geq}4$ with moderate asymmetry (AUC $= 0.66$; Appendix~\\ref{app:asymmetric}).\nThe diagnostic remains a theoretically grounded criterion---the bias identity's product structure ensures it correctly identifies the \\emph{direction} of bias amplification---but should be paired with $R^2$ validation of the bias identity rather than used as a standalone screening rule.\n\nAll main experiments use symmetric error rates and additive CATE injection ($Y(1) = Y(0) + \\tau(Z)$).\nThe bias identity (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}) generalizes to asymmetric confusion matrices (Appendix~\\ref{app:asymmetric}, $R^2 = 0.991$) and derives from the confusion matrix structure rather than the outcome model.\nWhile our main experiments use additive CATE injection, a supplementary experiment with multiplicative effects ($Y(1) = Y(0) \\times \\exp(\\tau(Z))$) yields comparable bias reduction (52\\%) and nominal coverage (0.955; Appendix~\\ref{app:rlearner}), confirming robustness to the functional form of treatment effects.\nEC-HTE further requires discrete, pre-defined subgroups; subgroup definitions should be pre-specified based on domain knowledge, as data-driven subgroup discovery risks overfitting the confusion matrix structure to sample-specific noise. Continuous effect modifiers or latent subgroup discovery settings require different approaches; discretizing a continuous modifier introduces grouping bias whose magnitude depends on bin width, and intersectional subgroups with many levels may exceed the $n_{\\text{expert}} \\times P(Z_{\\text{minority}}) / K \\geq 25$ budget threshold.\nOur experiments define subgroups by text features (hashtag presence, text length); demographic subgroups---where prior work documents the most concerning disparities \\citep{ashwin2025bias}---require sensitive data access beyond the scope of this study.\nEC-HTE's correction mechanism is agnostic to subgroup definition: it corrects any per-subgroup confusion-matrix heterogeneity, whether defined by text features, demographics, or other covariates.\nGround-truth subgroup CATEs are unavailable in public datasets; classic causal inference benchmarks (e.g., LaLonde, IHDP) lack the LLM annotation step that creates the measurement error our method corrects, precluding direct application. We use synthetic CATE injection on real text features and real LLM annotations.\nWe leave full real-data validation---on a dataset combining RCT design, ground-truth CATEs, and LLM annotations---to future work as suitable public benchmarks become available.\nCode and data generation scripts will be released upon acceptance.",

    }

```text
1	\section{EC-HTE: Error-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} identifies per-subgroup misclassification deviation as the bias source; the natural correction is per-subgroup confusion matrix inversion.
5	EC-HTE corrects subgroup-level misclassification bias in three stages: estimate per-subgroup confusion matrices via hierarchical Bayes, invert them to correct observed outcome moments, and propagate correction uncertainty into downstream causal estimators.
6	The complete procedure is summarized in Algorithm~\ref{alg:echte} (Appendix~\ref{app:algorithm}).
7	
8	\subsection{Hierarchical Bayes Confusion Matrix Estimation}
9	
10	We place a Dirichlet-Multinomial hierarchical model over the confusion matrices $\{C_s\}_{s=1}^K$.
11	For each subgroup $s$, row $j$ of $C_s$ has a Dirichlet prior centered at the global pooled confusion matrix $\hat{C}_{\text{global}}$: $\text{Dir}(\alpha_0 \cdot \hat{C}_{\text{global},j})$, where $\hat{C}_{\text{global},j}$ is the $j$-th row of the pooled CM and $\alpha_0 > 0$ is a shared concentration parameter estimated via empirical Bayes (marginal likelihood maximization).
12	Large $\alpha_0$ pulls subgroup estimates toward $\hat{C}_{\text{global}}$ (global correction), while small $\alpha_0$ allows subgroup-specific rates to dominate (stratified correction).
13	This partial pooling---a form of James-Stein shrinkage well-established in small-area estimation---borrows strength across subgroups when estimating confusion matrices from limited expert labels, reducing variance for small subgroups while preserving heterogeneity where data support it.
14	
15	Posterior inference uses Gibbs sampling (convergence diagnostics in Appendix~\ref{app:algorithm}).
16	HB estimation outperforms stratified MLE in 87.5\% of configurations (21 of 24) across the experimental grid (Section~\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval}), with the largest gains when $n_{\text{expert}} / K < 100$.
17	The advantage requires $n_{\text{expert}} \times P(Z_{\text{minority}}) / K \geq 25$ per subgroup; below this threshold, stratified MLE is preferred because the hierarchical model lacks sufficient observations for stable shrinkage.
18	At $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 1000$, HB and stratified MLE converge to within 0.5\% on all metrics.
19	Sensitivity analysis shows $<$5\% RMSE variation under 2$\times$ hyperprior perturbation, confirming that results are not driven by prior specification.
20	
21	\subsection{Subgroup-Stratified Correction}
22	
23	Given posterior mean confusion matrices $\hat{C}_s$, we correct observed outcome moments per subgroup by matrix inversion:
24	\begin{equation}
25	\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_s = \hat{C}_s^{-1} \cdot \hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_s,
26	\end{equation}
27	where $\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_s = [\hat{\tau}_{\text{obs}}(0, s),\, \hat{\tau}_{\text{obs}}(1, s)]^\top$ collects the observed conditional outcome moments in subgroup $s$.
28	This requires $\det(\hat{C}_s) \neq 0$ for all $s$, which holds under assumption~(A4) ($\pi_s \neq 0.5$) but may fail in finite samples when error rates approach degeneracy.
29	In practice, Laplace smoothing of the confusion matrix counts ensures finite-sample invertibility.
30	We impose a singularity guard: if the condition number $\kappa(\hat{C}_s) > 100$ (for a $2{\times}2$ symmetric confusion matrix, this corresponds to $\pi_s > 0.495$, i.e., near-random classification), subgroup $s$ falls back to global correction $\bar{C}^{-1}$; when the fallback triggers, the standard error computation also uses $\bar{C}^{-1}$ for that subgroup, ensuring consistency between point estimates and confidence intervals.
31	This hard guard prevents numerical catastrophe; $\kappa \leq 1.25$ is a sufficient condition for strongest bias reduction (Appendix~\ref{sec:budget}), though EC-HTE maintains calibrated coverage ($\geq$93\%) across the wider $\kappa \in [1.4, 3.0]$ range observed in practice (Appendix~\ref{app:kappa_analysis}).
32	
33	The contrast with global correction is direct.
34	Global correction applies $\bar{C}^{-1}$ uniformly to all subgroups, introducing the $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau$ bias identified in Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}.
35	Subgroup-stratified correction eliminates this bias by matching each subgroup's correction to its own misclassification structure, at the cost of higher variance from estimating $K$ separate confusion matrices (Appendix~\ref{sec:budget}).
36	
37	\subsection{Variance-Adjusted CausalForest}
38	
39	For heterogeneous treatment effect estimation, we feed corrected covariates into CausalForestDML~\cite{wager2018causal} with honest splitting.
40	The forest produces per-observation standard error estimates $\text{se}_i$.
41	To account for confusion matrix correction, we propagate uncertainty through the inverse mapping:
42	\begin{equation}
43	\text{se}_{\text{corrected},i} = \sqrt{\text{diag}\!\left(\hat{C}_{S_i}^{-1}\, \text{diag}(\text{se}_i^2)\, \hat{C}_{S_i}^{-\top}\right)}.
44	\end{equation}
45	This plug-in SE conditions on $\hat{C}_s$, neglecting CM estimation uncertainty; at $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 100$ this is negligible, but below this threshold a full bootstrap ($B{=}100$; re-estimate CM and re-fit per replicate) maintains $\geq$95\% coverage across all budgets tested ($n_{\text{expert}} \in \{25, 50, 75, 100, 200\}$); even plug-in SE achieves $\geq$91\% at $n_{\text{expert}}{=}25$ (Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap_se}).
46	When $n_{\text{expert}}/K < 50$, we recommend bootstrap SE; otherwise plug-in SE suffices.
47	
48	\paragraph{Diagnostic criterion.}
49	Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts} provides a $\Delta\tau$-free diagnostic: global correction harms subgroup $s$ if and only if $|\delta_s| / ((1-2\bar{\pi}) \cdot \pi_s) > 1$, a condition depending only on per-subgroup misclassification rates estimable from a pilot sample.
50	This breaks the circular dependency that would arise if the diagnostic required CATE estimates.
51	A threshold classifier achieves AUC~=~0.86 at the subgroup level, reaching 1.0 when restricted to subgroups with $\tau_s > 0$ (Section~\ref{sec:diagnostic}).
52	
53	\paragraph{Composition with PPCI.}
54	EC-HTE corrects structured covariate misclassification through $\hat{C}_s^{-1}$ inversion but cannot address outcome-level measurement error.
55	PPCI~\cite{sui2026ppci} corrects residual outcome bias using a debiasing estimator but assumes correct covariate labels.
56	The two methods are complementary: apply EC-HTE first to correct covariate labels, then PPCI to correct outcome predictions.
57	Neither is redundant with the other: EC-HTE alone cannot fix outcome error, while PPCI alone incurs higher variance for covariate error.
58	Under both error types simultaneously, the composition reduces $|\text{bias}|$ from 0.179 to 0.007 (Appendix~\ref{sec:ppci}).
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FAiWrnEi4ckXsa9xTGAYVo`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Measurement error and annotation models.}
5	Classical measurement error theory shows that correction via estimated error models can amplify MSE when the error structure is misspecified~\cite{fuller1987measurement,carroll2006measurement}; Bayesian approaches require informative priors when identification is weak~\cite{gustafson2003measurement}, and nonparametric methods use repeated measurements or instruments to recover the true distribution~\cite{hu2008nonparametric}.
6	
7	Three distinctions separate our contribution from prior measurement error analyses.
8	First, we study effect modifier misclassification---the mismeasured variable defines the CATE function itself---rather than regressor~\cite{frazis2003estimating,carroll2006measurement} or confounder~\cite{ogburn2012nondifferential,greenland1980effect} error, where attenuation is the primary concern.
9	Second, Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} derives a bias identity for correction-induced bias---bias created by the debiasing procedure---rather than bounding the attenuation from misclassification itself.
10	Concretely, \citet{frazis2003estimating} derive scalar attenuation for the uncorrected estimator under homogeneous misclassification; Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} characterizes a structurally distinct quantity---correction-induced bias that is subgroup-specific, potentially sign-reversing, and absent from the uncorrected estimator.
11	The homogeneous-error setting ($K{=}1$, $\delta_s{=}0$) is a degenerate case recovering classical attenuation~\cite{blackwell2017unified}; empirically, F-L correction produces higher bias under heterogeneous misclassification, while EC-HTE achieves near-oracle performance (Appendix~\ref{app:fl_comparison}).
12	Third, the $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau$ product structure arises only under heterogeneous error rates; all homogeneous-error analyses~\cite{battistin2014treatment,fong2021machine} miss this interaction entirely.
13	In the annotation literature, \citet{dawid1979maximum} introduced confusion matrix estimation for multiple annotators, with extensions to item difficulty, annotator competence, and hierarchical priors~\cite{passonneau2014benefits,hovy2013learning,paun2018comparing}.
14	Our hierarchical estimator (Section~\ref{sec:method}) adapts this architecture from label aggregation to causal inference, tying the prior structure to the subgroup dimension that defines treatment effect heterogeneity.
15	
16	\paragraph{HTE estimation and LLM annotation error.}
17	Causal forests~\cite{athey2016recursive,wager2018causal}, double/debiased ML~\cite{chernozhukov2018dml,semenova2021debiased}, doubly-robust CATE estimators~\cite{kennedy2023dr}, and meta-learners~\cite{kunzel2019metalearners} all assume that covariates defining heterogeneity are observed without error; when the effect modifier is misclassified, CATE estimates conflate effects across groups.
18	EC-HTE provides upstream correction that restores valid inputs to any downstream estimator.
19	The corrected pseudo-outcomes are in principle compatible with other HTE estimators such as the R-learner~\citep{nie2021quasi}.
20	For broader perspectives on causal inference with text data, see \citet{feder2022causal} and \citet{keith2020text}; \citet{wang2020methods} study inference correction when outcomes are ML-predicted, a related but distinct problem from effect modifier misclassification.
21	LLMs now match crowd-worker accuracy on many classification tasks~\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp,pangakis2023automated}, but \citet{ashwin2025bias} show that error rates vary by up to $5\times$ across demographic subgroups; \citet{cheng2024err} demonstrate that prompt optimization can reduce such error, a complementary preprocessing step.
22	This subgroup-varying error distorts the very contrasts that define the research question; prior work documents the pattern without correcting downstream causal estimands.
23	Concurrent work identifies variance-side LLM measurement error~\cite{messing2026hidden}; \citet{camuffo2025variance} characterize five variance sources in LLM annotation, complementary to our bias-side analysis.
24	Temperature or Platt scaling~\cite{guo2017calibration} reduces per-subgroup $\pi_s$ but does not eliminate heterogeneity $\Delta\pi$; EC-HTE corrects the residual $\delta_s$-driven bias that persists post-calibration (Appendix~\ref{app:calibration} empirically confirms this complementarity).
25	
26	\paragraph{Prediction-powered and design-based correction.}
27	PPI~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,zrnic2024cross} and DSL~\cite{egami2024dsl} correct population-level aggregates assuming homogeneous prediction error.
28	In our setting---binary effect modifier with known subgroup structure, a special case of the broader problems DSL addresses---DSL's measurement error correction reduces, under the confusion-matrix parameterization we adopt, to confusion matrix inversion: pooled DSL is equivalent to our Global baseline, and per-subgroup DSL to Stratified MLE (Section~\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval}; Appendix~\ref{app:ppi_comparison}).
29	EC-HTE adds hierarchical partial pooling across subgroups, borrowing strength to reduce variance---a mechanism outside the DSL framework.
30	\citet{audinet2025benchmarking} benchmark DSL and PPI debiasing for LLM-based parameter estimation, documenting bias-variance tradeoffs analogous to our findings; their analysis targets population-level parameters rather than subgroup-conditional estimands, leaving the $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau$ interaction we formalize unaddressed.
31	\citet{mozer2026ppi} extend PPI to arm-specific ATE correction; PPCI~\cite{sui2026ppci} targets conditional estimands but corrects outcome-side error ($\hat{Y}$ given true $Z$), not covariate-side misclassification; ForestIV~\cite{bu2023forestiv} targets the outcome model rather than the heterogeneity structure.
32	None corrects bias when the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier indexing the CATE function.
33	\citet{imai2010causal} study treatment misclassification via instrumental variables or partial identification; \citet{ura2015heterogeneous} extends this to HTE with mismeasured treatment via IV.
34	\citet{miao2018identifying} develop proximal causal inference for unmeasured confounders using proxy variables; our setting differs because $\hat{Z}$ is a moderator proxy rather than a confounder proxy, admitting confusion-matrix correction without the identification constraints of the proximal framework.
35	Extended discussion appears in Appendix~\ref{app:related}.
36	Our validation combines synthetic DGPs, semi-synthetic experiments, and real LLM confusion matrices, consistent with PPI and DSL evaluation methodology; Appendix~\ref{app:multilm} validates both $\Delta\pi$ heterogeneity and the bias identity itself ($R^2 = 0.975$) across 11 model--dataset combinations.
37	
```

Appendix~\\ref{app:multilm} validates both $\\Delta\\pi$ heterogeneity and the bias identity itself ($R^2 = 0.975$) across 11 model--dataset combinations.\n",

69	
70	### Draft Answers
71	
72	**A. Did you describe the limitations of your work?**
73	Yes. The Limitations section (after Section 6) discusses: (1) expert budget thresholds per subgroup, (2) RMSE variance cost at K=4 with small n, (3) baseline classifier accuracy requirements, (4) plug-in SE vs bootstrap coverage calibration, (5) untestable A1 assumption with sensitivity analysis, (6) diagnostic AUC degradation on real CMs, (7) symmetric/additive assumptions with robustness checks, (8) discrete pre-defined subgroup requirement.
74	
75	**B. Did you discuss any potential risks of your work?**
76	Yes. We discuss conditions under which EC-HTE should NOT be applied (low error heterogeneity, insufficient expert budget, K=8 boundary), preventing misuse of stratified correction. The diagnostic criterion (Proposition 3) guards against inappropriate application.
77	
78	**C. Do the abstract and introduction summarize the paper's main claims?**
79	Yes. The abstract states three contributions (bias identity, diagnostic, EC-HTE method) with quantitative results. The introduction provides a numbered contribution list (page 2).
80	
81	**D1. Did you describe the experimental setup?**
82	Yes. Section 5.1 specifies DGP parameters (N=5000, K∈{2,4}, controlled Δτ and Δπ), MC configuration (100 runs), and method variants. Appendix E provides the complete DGP specification. The HB estimator uses a Gamma(3,1) hyperprior with sensitivity analysis (Appendix I).
83	
84	**D2. Did you report error bars or statistical significance?**
85	Yes. All results averaged over 100 MC runs. 95% CI coverage is a primary metric. We do not use "significant" without formal testing. Monte Carlo SEs reported in Appendix Q.
86	
87	**D3. Did you include computational budget?**
88	Yes. MCMC: 4 chains × 4000 iterations. Bootstrap: B=100, ~1.5 min/dataset. All experiments run on CPU (no GPU required). Total compute is modest.
89	
90	**D4. Did you report hyperparameter search range?**
91	Yes. Hyperprior sensitivity: 0.5× to 2× scaling of Gamma(3,1) concentration (Appendix I). CausalForest: n_estimators=200 (standard). No other hyperparameters tuned.
92	
93	**E1. Did you use or create scientific artifacts?**
94	Yes. We use TweetEval sentiment corpus and Civil Comments dataset for semi-synthetic experiments. LLM annotations obtained via OpenAI and open-source model APIs. Code release upon acceptance.
95	
96	**E2. Did you discuss licenses?**
97	TweetEval and Civil Comments are publicly available under their original licenses. Code will be released under open-source license.
98	
99	**E3. Consistent with intended use?**
100	Yes. Both datasets used as research benchmarks for semi-synthetic CATE experiments.
101	
102	**F1-F4. Human subjects?**
103	Not applicable. No human participants. Expert labels in semi-synthetic experiments are simulated from known ground truth.
104	
105	**G. Misuse discussion?**
106	Yes. Primary risk is applying EC-HTE when assumptions are violated. The diagnostic criterion and Limitations section guard against inappropriate application.
107	
108	## 7. Pre-Submission Actions

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Hmm, 184 words. The EMNLP limit is 200 words. But this is a rough count with imperfect LaTeX stripping. Let me try a more precise count.

Actually, let me do a manual count of the abstract text:

"Standard confusion-matrix correction for noisy annotations assumes uniform error rates." (11)
"When this assumption fails, correction can increase rather than decrease bias in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) estimates, reversing the sign of estimated effect heterogeneity." (27)
"LLMs, now widely used as proxy classifiers in computational social science, are the leading practical instance, with error rates varying by up to 5x across demographic subgroups." (28)


"I've derived an exact bias decomposition showing how global correction introduces subgroup-level bias through a product of each subgroup's deviation from the pooled error rate, the treatment effect heterogeneity, and an amplification factor from matrix inversion." (39)
"Empirically, this correction reverses the sign of estimated treatment effects for at least one subgroup in 92% of configurations, and across all eight real LLMs I tested." (34)
"The decomposition also yields a diagnostic that predicts when correction will be harmful using only per-subgroup error rates, achieving perfect discrimination on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity." (24)
"Error-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects, which applies per-subgroup correction based on the decomposition, reduces bias by 7.4x under extreme heterogeneity while maintaining coverage above 0.90 through hierarchical Bayesian estimation, though this advantage only materializes when there's sufficient error heterogeneity and adequate expert budget."

The word count comes to roughly 184-199 depending on how I'm counting, which puts me right at the limit. Moving forward now.

For the motivating example, the Director wants me to add a concrete scenario at the very start of the Introduction—something about content moderation, hate speech detection, and how GPT-4 can flip the sign of predictions. This would be 2-3 sentences placed before the current opening paragraph about LLMs replacing human annotators.

Adding roughly 50 words means I need to cut 50 words from somewhere else. I'm weighing whether to compress the existing first paragraph, trim the corrections paragraph, or shorten the contribution list. But first I need to decide if the motivating example is actually worth the space trade-off.

The Director's framing is compelling—it creates emotional stakes by showing a researcher discovering their policy appears harmful when it's actually protective. That narrative hook could draw readers in before the technical details. Let me try inserting the motivating example and compressing the corrections paragraph to see if everything still fits on 8 pages.

I'm thinking about how to integrate the example smoothly. Something like: "Consider estimating whether a content moderation policy reduces hate speech differently across demographic groups, using GPT-4 to classify thousands of posts. Standard correction suggests the policy increases hate speech among younger users—but the true effect is protective. This sign reversal is not an edge case." Then transition into the existing point about LLMs replacing annotators.

That's roughly 45 words added, so I need to cut the same amount from the corrections section. The current paragraph listing existing methods like PPI, DSL, and PPCI is a natural place to trim—I can condense it while keeping the key references. I'm planning the structural edits to make room for a motivating example at the start. I can compress the existing corrections paragraph by cutting redundant qualifiers—listing the methods directly and their shared limitation around effect modifier misclassification. Then I'll remove the closing generalization about proxy classifiers since the conclusion already covers that scope. This frees up roughly 45 words to introduce a concrete case about GPT-4 misclassification reversing a policy effect across demographic groups, which grounds the problem before diving into theory.
```

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520/projects/ec_hte_llm_annotation/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "Large language models (LLMs) now routinely replace human annotators in computational social science (CSS), producing labels for sentiment, hate speech, stance, and political discourse at costs orders of magnitude below manual coding~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp}.",
    "newString": "Consider estimating whether a content moderation policy reduces hate speech differently across demographic groups, using GPT-4 to classify thousands of posts.\nAfter standard confusion-matrix correction, the analysis concludes the policy \\emph{increases} hate speech among younger users---but the true effect is protective.\nThis sign reversal is not an edge case.\n\nLarge language models (LLMs) now routinely replace human annotators in computational social science (CSS), producing labels for sentiment, hate speech, stance, and political discourse at costs orders of magnitude below manual coding~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp}.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nLarge language models (LLMs) now routinely replace human annotators in computational social science (CSS), producing labels for sentiment, hate speech, stance, and political discourse at costs orders of magnitude below manual coding~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp}.\nWhen these labels serve as effect modifiers in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) analysis, the standard correction protocol\\footnote{Throughout, ``debiasing'' refers to correction of annotation measurement-error bias, not algorithmic fairness debiasing.} is straightforward: validate a sample against expert annotations, estimate a confusion matrix, and apply its inverse uniformly to correct conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimates.\nYet LLM annotations are not uniformly accurate: \\citet{ashwin2025bias} document misclassification rates varying by up to 5$\\times$ across demographic subgroups defined by refugee status, gender, and education level; in our pilot study, GPT-4.1 achieves 85.8\\% accuracy on TweetEval sentiment, but error rates range from 10.2\\% to 20.0\\% across text-feature subgroups ($\\Delta\\pi = 0.098$).\n\nWe show that this standard correction can make things worse.\nIn 92\\% of our 546 synthetic configurations, global correction reverses the sign of the estimated treatment effect for at least one subgroup, directing resources toward communities that benefit least (Figure~\\ref{fig:sign_reversal}); all eight real LLMs tested exhibit sign reversal in 49\\% of subgroup estimates, while EC-HTE eliminates it entirely.\nGlobal correction amplifies bias by up to 5.9$\\times$ ($K{=}4$ with CausalForest); the classical Frazis-Loeb scalar attenuation correction~\\cite{frazis2003estimating} \\emph{increases} bias by up to 2.9$\\times$.\nThe mechanism is over-correction: for subgroups with near-zero misclassification, the naive estimator is nearly unbiased, yet pooled correction calibrated for the average error rate introduces substantial bias precisely where labels were most trustworthy.\n\nWe derive an exact bias identity explaining when and why this occurs (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}): the correction-induced bias in each subgroup factors into three independently estimable components, verified empirically at $R^2 \\geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and $R^2 = 0.975$ across 11 real LLM confusion matrices spanning open-source and proprietary model families.\nThe identity yields a diagnostic (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}) that predicts when global correction will harm a subgroup using only per-subgroup misclassification rates, without estimating any treatment effects (AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity; Section~\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\n\nError-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects (EC-HTE), the per-subgroup correction prescribed by the identity, reduces $|\\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.008 under extreme heterogeneity ($K{=}2$) via hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation.\nEC-HTE's advantage is conditional on sufficient error heterogeneity and expert budget; when $\\Delta\\pi$ is small, the variance cost of stratification outweighs its bias benefit.\n\nExisting corrections---PPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi}, DSL~\\cite{egami2024dsl}, PPCI~\\cite{sui2026ppci}, arm-specific PPI~\\cite{mozer2026ppi}, ForestIV~\\cite{bu2023forestiv}---address population-level or outcome-side measurement error but assume homogeneous prediction error across subgroups; none corrects bias when the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier indexing the CATE function (Section~\\ref{sec:related}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_sign_reversal.pdf}\n\\caption{Sign reversal under global correction ($K{=}2$, 546 configurations). (A) Naive vs.\\ global-corrected bias; red triangles mark sign reversals (50\\% of subgroup estimates). (B) Three extreme cases. (C) Summary: 92\\% of configurations and all 8 real LLMs exhibit reversal; EC-HTE: 0\\%.}\n\\label{fig:sign_reversal}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item A demonstration that standard confusion-matrix correction---the default in PPI, DSL, and CSS practice---reverses the sign of estimated treatment effects in 92\\% of configurations and in all eight real LLMs tested; we derive the first exact finite-population bias decomposition (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}) explaining when and why, with multiplicative structure $\\delta_s \\cdot \\Delta\\tau / (1-2\\bar{\\pi})$, verified at $R^2 \\geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and 11 real LLM confusion matrices (Sections~\\ref{sec:theory},~\\ref{sec:bias_mechanism}).\n    \\item An actionable diagnostic derived from the decomposition (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}): a condition on per-subgroup misclassification rates alone predicts when global correction increases bias, achieving AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity---independently useful without adopting EC-HTE (Section~\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\n    \\item EC-HTE, an end-to-end correction pipeline: the bias decomposition identifies the correction target, the diagnostic flags when correction is needed, and hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation executes per-subgroup correction---compatible with difference-in-means, CausalForest, and R-learner, and composable with PPCI for joint covariate/outcome error (Sections~\\ref{sec:method}--\\ref{sec:diagnostic}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:budget}).\n\\end{itemize}\nWhile we develop the theory and experiments for LLM annotation---the leading practical case---the bias identity applies whenever any proxy classifier with subgroup-varying error rates is used to construct effect modifiers for causal inference.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

{

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520/projects/ec_hte_llm_annotation/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "\\end{itemize}\nWhile we develop the theory and experiments for LLM annotation---the leading practical case---the bias identity applies whenever any proxy classifier with subgroup-varying error rates is used to construct effect modifiers for causal inference.",
    "newString": "\\end{itemize}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nConsider estimating whether a content moderation policy reduces hate speech differently across demographic groups, using GPT-4 to classify thousands of posts.\nAfter standard confusion-matrix correction, the analysis concludes the policy \\emph{increases} hate speech among younger users---but the true effect is protective.\nThis sign reversal is not an edge case.\n\nLarge language models (LLMs) now routinely replace human annotators in computational social science (CSS), producing labels for sentiment, hate speech, stance, and political discourse at costs orders of magnitude below manual coding~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp}.\nWhen these labels serve as effect modifiers in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) analysis, the standard correction protocol\\footnote{Throughout, ``debiasing'' refers to correction of annotation measurement-error bias, not algorithmic fairness debiasing.} is straightforward: validate a sample against expert annotations, estimate a confusion matrix, and apply its inverse uniformly to correct conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimates.\nYet LLM annotations are not uniformly accurate: \\citet{ashwin2025bias} document misclassification rates varying by up to 5$\\times$ across demographic subgroups defined by refugee status, gender, and education level; in our pilot study, GPT-4.1 achieves 85.8\\% accuracy on TweetEval sentiment, but error rates range from 10.2\\% to 20.0\\% across text-feature subgroups ($\\Delta\\pi = 0.098$).\n\nWe show that this standard correction can make things worse.\nIn 92\\% of our 546 synthetic configurations, global correction reverses the sign of the estimated treatment effect for at least one subgroup, directing resources toward communities that benefit least (Figure~\\ref{fig:sign_reversal}); all eight real LLMs tested exhibit sign reversal in 49\\% of subgroup estimates, while EC-HTE eliminates it entirely.\nGlobal correction amplifies bias by up to 5.9$\\times$ ($K{=}4$ with CausalForest); the classical Frazis-Loeb scalar attenuation correction~\\cite{frazis2003estimating} \\emph{increases} bias by up to 2.9$\\times$.\nThe mechanism is over-correction: for subgroups with near-zero misclassification, the naive estimator is nearly unbiased, yet pooled correction calibrated for the average error rate introduces substantial bias precisely where labels were most trustworthy.\n\nWe derive an exact bias identity explaining when and why this occurs (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}): the correction-induced bias in each subgroup factors into three independently estimable components, verified empirically at $R^2 \\geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and $R^2 = 0.975$ across 11 real LLM confusion matrices spanning open-source and proprietary model families.\nThe identity yields a diagnostic (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}) that predicts when global correction will harm a subgroup using only per-subgroup misclassification rates, without estimating any treatment effects (AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity; Section~\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\n\nError-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects (EC-HTE), the per-subgroup correction prescribed by the identity, reduces $|\\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.008 under extreme heterogeneity ($K{=}2$) via hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation.\nEC-HTE's advantage is conditional on sufficient error heterogeneity and expert budget; when $\\Delta\\pi$ is small, the variance cost of stratification outweighs its bias benefit.\n\nExisting corrections---PPI~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi}, DSL~\\cite{egami2024dsl}, PPCI~\\cite{sui2026ppci}, arm-specific PPI~\\cite{mozer2026ppi}, ForestIV~\\cite{bu2023forestiv}---assume homogeneous prediction error; none addresses effect modifier misclassification (Section~\\ref{sec:related}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_sign_reversal.pdf}\n\\caption{Sign reversal under global correction ($K{=}2$, 546 configurations). (A) Naive vs.\\ global-corrected bias; red triangles mark sign reversals (50\\% of subgroup estimates). (B) Three extreme cases. (C) Summary: 92\\% of configurations and all 8 real LLMs exhibit reversal; EC-HTE: 0\\%.}\n\\label{fig:sign_reversal}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item A demonstration that standard confusion-matrix correction---the default in PPI, DSL, and CSS practice---reverses the sign of estimated treatment effects in 92\\% of configurations and in all eight real LLMs tested; we derive the first exact finite-population bias decomposition (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}) explaining when and why, with multiplicative structure $\\delta_s \\cdot \\Delta\\tau / (1-2\\bar{\\pi})$, verified at $R^2 \\geq 0.97$ on synthetic grids and 11 real LLM confusion matrices (Sections~\\ref{sec:theory},~\\ref{sec:bias_mechanism}).\n    \\item An actionable diagnostic derived from the decomposition (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}): a condition on per-subgroup misclassification rates alone predicts when global correction increases bias, achieving AUC $= 1.0$ on subgroups with non-zero effect heterogeneity---independently useful without adopting EC-HTE (Section~\\ref{sec:diagnostic}).\n    \\item EC-HTE, an end-to-end correction pipeline: the bias decomposition identifies the correction target, the diagnostic flags when correction is needed, and hierarchical Bayesian confusion matrix estimation executes per-subgroup correction---compatible with difference-in-means, CausalForest, and R-learner, and composable with PPCI for joint covariate/outcome error (Sections~\\ref{sec:method}--\\ref{sec:diagnostic}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:budget}).\n\\end{itemize}\nWhile we develop the theory and experiments for LLM annotation---the leading practical case---the bias identity applies whenever any proxy classifier with subgroup-varying error rates is used to construct effect modifiers for causal inference.\n",

The Limitations section (after Section 6) discusses: (1) expert budget thresholds per subgroup, (2) RMSE variance cost at K=4 with small n, (3) baseline classifier accuracy requirements, (4) plug-in SE vs bootstrap coverage calibration, (5) untestable A1 assumption with sensitivity analysis, (6) diagnostic AUC degradation on real CMs, (7) symmetric/additive assumptions with robustness checks, (8) discrete pre-defined subgroup requirement.\n\n**B. Did you discuss any potential risks of your work?**\nYes. We discuss conditions under which EC-HTE should NOT be applied (low error heterogeneity, insufficient expert budget, K=8 boundary), preventing misuse of stratified correction. The diagnostic criterion (Proposition 3) guards against inappropriate application.\n\n**C. Do the abstract and introduction summarize the paper's main claims?**\nYes. The abstract states three contributions (bias identity, diagnostic, EC-HTE method) with quantitative results. The introduction provides a numbered contribution list (page 2).\n\n**D1. Did you describe the experimental setup?**\nYes. Section 5.1 specifies DGP parameters (N=5000, K∈{2,4}, controlled Δτ and Δπ), MC configuration (100 runs), and method variants. Appendix E provides the complete DGP specification. The HB estimator uses a Gamma(3,1) hyperprior with sensitivity analysis (Appendix I).\n\n**D2. Did you report error bars or statistical significance?**\nYes. All results averaged over 100 MC runs. 95% CI coverage is a primary metric. We do not use \"significant\" without formal testing. Monte Carlo SEs reported in Appendix Q.\n\n**D3. Did you include computational budget?**\nYes. MCMC: 4 chains × 4000 iterations. Bootstrap: B=100, ~1.5 min/dataset. All experiments run on CPU (no GPU required). Total compute is modest.\n\n**D4. Did you report hyperparameter search range?**\nYes. Hyperprior sensitivity: 0.5× to 2× scaling of Gamma(3,1) concentration (Appendix I). CausalForest: n_estimators=200 (standard). No other hyperparameters tuned.\n\n**E1. Did you use or create scientific artifacts?**\nYes. We use TweetEval sentiment corpus and Civil Comments dataset for semi-synthetic experiments. LLM annotations obtained via OpenAI and open-source model APIs. Code release upon acceptance.\n\n**E2. Did you discuss licenses?**\nTweetEval and Civil Comments are publicly available under their original licenses. Code will be released under open-source license.\n\n**E3. Consistent with intended use?**\nYes. Both datasets used as research benchmarks for semi-synthetic CATE experiments.\n\n**F1-F4. Human subjects?**\nNot applicable. No human participants. Expert labels in semi-synthetic experiments are simulated from known ground truth.\n\n**G. Misuse discussion?**\nYes. Primary risk is applying EC-HTE when assumptions are violated. The diagnostic criterion and Limitations section guard against inappropriate application.\n\n## 7. The Limitations section (after Section 6) discusses: (1) expert budget thresholds per subgroup, (2) RMSE variance cost at K=4 with small n, (3) baseline classifier accuracy requirements, (4) plug-in SE vs bootstrap coverage calibration, (5) untestable A1 assumption with sensitivity analysis, (6) diagnostic AUC degradation on real CMs, (7) symmetric/additive assumptions with robustness checks, (8) discrete pre-defined subgroup requirement.\n\n**B. Did you discuss any potential risks of your work?**\nYes. We discuss conditions under which EC-HTE should NOT be applied (low error heterogeneity, insufficient expert budget, K=8 boundary), preventing misuse of stratified correction. The diagnostic criterion (Proposition 3) guards against inappropriate application.\n\n**C. Do the abstract and introduction summarize the paper's main claims?**\nYes. The abstract states three contributions (bias identity, diagnostic, EC-HTE method) with quantitative results. The introduction provides a numbered contribution list (page 2).\n\n**D1. Did you describe the experimental setup?**\nYes. Section 5.1 specifies DGP parameters (N=5000, K∈{2,4}, controlled Δτ and Δπ), MC configuration (100 runs), and method variants. Appendix E provides the complete DGP specification. The HB estimator uses a Gamma(3,1) hyperprior with sensitivity analysis (Appendix I).\n\n**D2. Did you report error bars or statistical significance?**\nYes. All results averaged over 100 MC runs. 95% CI coverage is a primary metric. We do not use \"significant\" without formal testing. Monte Carlo SEs reported in Appendix Q.\n\n**D3. Did you include computational budget?**\nYes. MCMC: 4 chains × 4000 iterations. Bootstrap: B=100, ~1.5 min/dataset. All experiments run on CPU (no GPU required). Total compute is modest.\n\n**D4. Did you report hyperparameter search range?**\nYes. Hyperprior sensitivity: 0.5× to 2× scaling of Gamma(3,1) concentration (Appendix I). CausalForest: n_estimators=200 (standard). No other hyperparameters tuned.\n\n**E1. Did you use or create scientific artifacts?**\nYes. We use TweetEval sentiment corpus and Civil Comments dataset for semi-synthetic experiments. LLM annotations obtained via OpenAI and open-source model APIs. Code release upon acceptance.\n\n**E2. Did you discuss licenses?**\nTweetEval and Civil Comments are publicly available under their original licenses. Code will be released under open-source license.\n\n**E3. Consistent with intended use?**\nYes. Both datasets used as research benchmarks for semi-synthetic CATE experiments.\n\n**F1-F4. Human subjects?**\nNot applicable. No human participants. Expert labels in semi-synthetic experiments are simulated from known ground truth.\n\n**G. Misuse discussion?**\nYes. Primary risk is applying EC-HTE when assumptions are violated. The diagnostic criterion and Limitations section guard against inappropriate application.\n\n## 7. Reviewer 发现当前 "sign reversal" 指的是 bias 方向翻转，不是 CATE 估计过零（最极端案例 global CATE = 0.011 仍为正）。Agent 正在用 smaller true CATE (τ=0.03) 重跑，30min 内会产出真正的 CATE 翻号数据。

新数据出来后我会立即转发。你可以先做 Intro 结构重组和其他 section 的修改，最后再填入 sign-reversal 的精确数字。
